	+ The word "virtue" now sounds out of date and moralistic
	+ This devaluation has led to a lack of understanding of what virtue means

• **Aristotle's definition of virtue**
	+ Virtue is the way of behaving that is most conducive to happiness in life
	+ Vice is the way of behaving that is least conducive to happiness
	+ Aristotle's Nicomachean Ethics studied the virtues and vices in a balanced way

• **The problem with modern relativism**
	+ Modern relativism asserts that making judgments about how to live is impossible
	+ There is no real good, and no true virtue (as these are relative)
	+ Relativism's closest approximation to virtue is "tolerance"

• **The dangers of virtue signalling**
	+ Telling people you're virtuous is not a virtue, but self-promotion
	+ Virtue signalling is a common vice
	+ Intolerance of others' views is not simply wrong, but a sign of unsophistication or danger

	+ Level 4: Lobsters engage in extreme combat, with a high risk of injury or death

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

• **Dr. Simons' Demonstrations**
	+ Showed people a field of wheat and slowly added a road cutting through it
	+ People often failed to notice the road, even though it occupied a third of the image
	+ Demonstrated how our brains can be tricked into ignoring important visual information

• **The Famous Gorilla Study**
	+ Dr. Simons showed people a video of two teams playing a game
	+ One team was wearing white shirts, the other was wearing black shirts
	+ Participants were asked to count the number of times the white shirts threw the ball to each other

• **The Unexpected Twist**
	+ A man in a gorilla suit appeared in the middle of the game for a few seconds
	+ Most participants missed the gorilla the first time they saw the video
	+ The gorilla was large and clearly visible on the screen

• **Why We Missed the Gorilla**
	+ Our brains are wired to focus on specific tasks and ignore everything else
	+ We use our high-resolution vision to focus on a few specific things and ignore the rest
	+ This is because vision is expensive and our brains need to prioritize what to pay attention to

• **The Cost of Vision**
	+ Our retina has limited high-resolution fovea cells, which are used for tasks like identifying faces
	+ Each foveal cell requires 10,000 cells in the visual cortex to process
	+ This means we need to triage our vision and focus on what's important

• **Peripheral Vision**
	+ Most of our vision is peripheral and low-resolution
	+ We use our peripheral vision to get a general sense of what's going on around us
	+ We focus our high-resolution vision on specific tasks and ignore the rest

• **The Gorilla Study Results**
	+ Most participants missed the gorilla the first time they saw the video
	+ The gorilla was clearly visible on the screen, but participants were focused on the ball game
	+ This demonstrates how our brains can be tricked into ignoring important visual information

• **The Counter Study**
	+ Dr. Simons showed people a video of someone being served at a counter
	+ The server disappeared and a different person appeared in their place
	+ Most participants didn't notice the change, even though it was a different person

• **Why We Missed the Change**
	+ Our brains are wired to focus on specific tasks and ignore everything else
	+ We use our high-resolution vision to focus on a few specific things and ignore the rest
	+ This is because vision is expensive and our brains need to prioritize what to pay attention to

• **The Implications**
	+ Our brains are capable of ignoring important visual information if we're not paying attention
	+ This can lead to missed opportunities and mistakes
	+ It's essential to be aware of our own limitations and biases when it comes to perception.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

• **Christianity's Impact on Society**
	+ Emphasized the inherent worth of every soul, regardless of social status
	+ Challenged the idea that salvation could be obtained through effort or worth ("works")
	+ Prevented the wealthy and powerful from morally dominating the commoner

• **The End of Slavery**
	+ Christianity's influence led to the reevaluation of slavery as a morally wrong practice
	+ The idea that owning slaves degraded the owner, not just the slave, was a radical concept
	+ This idea was difficult to grasp, as the opposite was previously self-evident

• **Christianity's Moral Revolution**
	+ Recognized the rights of all people, regardless of social status
	+ Held that sovereigns and states were morally charged to recognize those rights
	+ Separated church from state, preventing emperors from claiming divine authority

• **Christianity's Impact on Social Issues**
	+ Objected to infanticide, prostitution, and the principle that might makes right
	+ Insisted that women were as valuable as men
	+ Demanded that even enemies be regarded as human

• **The Progress of the Christian Revolution**
	+ Initially, the impossible problems of slavery and social inequality were solved
	+ As the solution was implemented, the problems disappeared from view
	+ New problems, less amenable to quick solution, came to occupy a central place in Western consciousness

• **The Development of Science**
	+ Was motivated by the remaining problems in Christianized societies
	+ Aimed to resolve corporeal and material suffering
	+ Led to the development of new technologies and innovations

• **The Legacy of Christianity**
	+ Has had a profound impact on Western society and law
	+ Has established the inherent worth of every soul as a fundamental presupposition
	+ Has challenged the idea that might makes right and that the powerful should dominate the weak

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

• **Bachofen's Theory**
	+ Proposed that humanity passed through a series of developmental stages
	+ First stage: Matriarchal society where women held power and polyamory was common
	+ Second stage: Dionysian phase, where power was taken by men
	+ Third stage: Apollonian phase, where patriarchy reigns and women belong to one man

• **Influence of Bachofen's Ideas**
	+ Became influential in certain circles, despite lack of historical evidence
	+ Archaeologist Marija Gimbutas claimed a peaceful goddess-and-woman-centred culture existed in Neolithic Europe
	+ Art historian Merlin Stone made similar claims in his book "When God Was a Woman"

• **Criticism of Bachofen's Ideas**
	+ Feminist Cynthia Eller called Bachofen's ideas "an ennobling lie"
	+ Eller's book "The Myth of Matriarchal Prehistory" criticized the theology of the women's movement

• **Jung's Analysis**
	+ Encountered Bachofen's ideas, but realized they represented a psychological rather than historical reality
	+ Saw Bachofen's thought as a projection of imaginative fantasy onto the external world

• **Neumann's Extension of Jung's Ideas**
	+ Traced the emergence of consciousness, symbolically masculine, and contrasted it with its symbolically feminine, material origins
	+ Subsumed Freud's theory of Oedipal parenting into a broader archetypal model

• **The Terrible Mother**
	+ An ancient symbol of the spirit of careless unconsciousness
	+ Manifests itself in various forms, including Tiamat in the Mesopotamian Epic of Gilgamesh

• **The Terrible Mother's Role**
	+ Tempts the spirit of awareness and enlightenment down into the protective womb-like embrace of the underworld
	+ Inspires self-consciousness, undermines courage, and fosters feelings of nihilism and hatred

• **The Terrible Mother in Modern Society**
	+ Can be seen in the too-tight embrace of too-caring mom
	+ Can also be seen in the fear of attractive women, who represent nature itself

• **The Struggle for Enlightenment**
	+ Consciousness struggles upwards towards the light, but is constantly tempted to sink back down into dependency and unconsciousness
	+ Is aided in this desire by anything that opposes enlightenment, articulation, rationality, self-determination, strength, and competence

• **The Importance of Self-Determination**
	+ Self-determination is essential for the development of consciousness and the avoidance of unconsciousness
	+ Overprotection and too-caring behavior can smother and devour the spirit of awareness and enlightenment

• **The Transformation of Social Policy**
	+ The Oedipal familial nightmare is rapidly being transformed into social policy
	+ This transformation can have negative consequences for the development of consciousness and the avoidance of unconsciousness.

• **Minimal Group Identification**
	+ The author mentions a phenomenon called "minimal group identification" discovered by social psychologist Henri Tajfel
	+ Tajfel's research showed that people tend to favor their own group members and reject others

• **Tajfel's Experiment**
	+ Tajfel brought people into his lab and asked them to estimate the quantity of dots on a screen
	+ He then categorized them into groups and asked them to divide money among the members

• **Group Identification**
	+ Tajfel's research showed that people tend to identify with their own group and favor its members
	+ This can lead to antisocial behavior towards members of other groups

• **Why Group Identification Occurs**
	+ The author suggests that group identification may be a solution to a complex problem of optimization
	+ This problem arises when two or more factors are important, but none can be maximized without diminishing the others

• **Cooperation and Competition**
	+ The author notes that cooperation and competition are both socially and psychologically desirable
	+ However, they can be in conflict with each other, and group identification may be a way to resolve this conflict

• **Conclusion**
	+ The author concludes that people are social and antisocial at the same time
	+ Group identification is a complex phenomenon that may be driven by a desire to optimize conflicting factors.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

• **Freud's Non-Judgmentalism**
	+ Freud recommended therapists be tolerant and non-judgmental to help patients feel comfortable and honest.
	+ This allowed patients to explore their unconscious thoughts and feelings, and discover their own moral judgments.
	+ However, Freud did not argue that people should live without making judgments or morality.

• **Freud's View on Morality**
	+ Freud believed that civilization arises when restraining rules and morality are in place.
	+ He argued that people are both more immoral and more moral than they are aware of.

• **The Yin/Yang Symbol**
	+ The yin/yang symbol is part of the five-part tajitu diagram, representing the original absolute unity and its division into the multiplicity of the observed world.
	+ The symbol is also discussed in Rule 2, below.

• **The Concept of Being**
	+ Being (with a capital "B") refers to the totality of human experience, including emotions, drives, dreams, and private thoughts.
	+ Being is shaped by our decisions and choices, and is not easily reducible to material and objective factors.

• **Heidegger's Influence**
	+ The author was influenced by the ideas of Martin Heidegger, a 20th-century German philosopher.
	+ Heidegger's concept of Being is central to the author's discussion of human experience.

• **The Five-Part Tajitu**
	+ The five-part tajitu diagram represents the origin of the cosmos as undifferentiated absolute, then dividing into yin and yang, and then into the five agents and the ten thousand things.
	+ Similar diagrams are used in other cultures, such as the Star of David and the Hindu hexagram.

• **The Symbolism of the Tajitu**
	+ The downward triangle symbolizes Shakti, the feminine, and the upward triangle symbolizes Shiva, the masculine.
	+ The two components are known as om and hrim in Sanskrit.

• **The Concept of Non-Judgmentalism**
	+ The author argues that non-judgmentalism is a powerful and liberating technique in therapy, but not in everyday life.
	+ People need to make judgments and live with morality in order to function in society.

• **The Importance of Responsibility**
	+ The author argues that people should treat themselves like someone they are responsible for helping.
	+ This means being kind, compassionate, and non-judgmental towards oneself.

• **The Interconnectedness of Concepts**
	+ The author notes that various cultural and philosophical concepts, such as the tajitu and the Star of David, share similar themes and symbolism.
	+ This highlights the interconnectedness of human experience and the importance of understanding different perspectives.
